Discussion Summary: Nadine was not present on call, but was joined by Qui Pham. Qui speaks French with some limited English. Since we did not have a translator, we covered a few items at a high-level with slides to support discussion to help with understanding/translation.  To develop a chatbot solution similar to Ask Gartner, utilizing retrieval augmented generation architecture, and ensure it is accessible to internal resources, employees, and citizens.

Summary:
- The discussion focused on the customer's interest in developing a chatbot similar to Ask Gartner, with an emphasis on understanding the underlying technology and architecture.
- The Gartner Expert explained the concept of retrieval augmented generation (RAG) architecture, highlighting its difference from standard large language models like ChatGPT.
- It was noted that structuring and preparing data effectively is crucial for the chatbot's performance, and the expert offered to provide additional resources for further understanding.
- The customer expressed intent to make the chatbot available to both internal employees and the public, necessitating careful management of access permissions and content visibility.
- Recommendations included ensuring the chatbot can provide source information and reference documents to enhance user trust and verification.
- The expert advised rolling out the chatbot internally first to allow employees to familiarize themselves with it before public deployment, facilitating smoother communication and troubleshooting.
- Transparency about the chatbot's capabilities and limitations was recommended, acknowledging that responses might not always be entirely accurate.
- Survey data was shared, indicating that while a significant portion of users are open to using AI chatbots, many still prefer human interaction due to trust and accuracy concerns.
- The importance of designing the chatbot with a fallback mechanism to human support was emphasized, ensuring users feel their time is valued even if the chatbot cannot resolve their issue.
- The customer opted to receive materials for offline reading to better understand the information discussed, with the possibility of a follow-up session.

Key takeaways:
- Brett will send an email with the presentation and additional reading materials to Qui Pham.
- Brett will provide links to research on building a chatbot similar to Ask Gartner.
- Brett will send more information on data structuring and preparation for the chatbot.

- He expressed a hypothesis that the market will stratify similar to the cloud market, with dominant tier one players like Open AI, Anthropic, and Gemini due to high switching costs and differentiated capabilities.
- Chirag suggested that AI models will need to cannibalize parts of the software industry to create stable revenue pools, as current subscription models are insufficient for long-term sustainability.
- He categorized the software market into low, medium, and high-risk disruption levels, with single-task experiences like CRM and content creation being highly susceptible to disruption.
- Chirag argued that while current AI tools are immature, the software industry must transition to AI-native experiences to survive, with companies like Adobe and Shopify at higher risk of disruption.
- He recommended software companies extend into business context layers and partner with AI providers to create programmatic hooks, thus maintaining relevance and revenue.
- Cheng Hua asked about the trend of traditional SaaS companies partnering with AI firms, to which Chirag affirmed this as a win-win strategy for both parties.
- Chirag noted that while partnerships are beneficial in the short term, AI companies may eventually cannibalize partners by developing domain-specific capabilities.
- He expressed concerns about Shopify's future, likening it to Adobe's, due to potential disintermediation by AI-driven shopping experiences.
- Chirag discussed the role of consulting firms like Accenture, indicating they are currently essential for scaling AI solutions but may face disruption as AI capabilities improve.
- He emphasized the economic incentives for AI companies to minimize intermediaries, predicting a future of stratification and cannibalization in the market.
- The conversation concluded with an acknowledgment of the ongoing evolution of AI models and the need for companies to adapt to remain competitive.

Date: 9-Mar-26
Reference Number: 19336913
Buyer Region: NORTHAM
Buyer Industry: Services
Buyer Role: IT/Infrastructure and Operations - Other Role
Purpose: Securing MS Copilot
Question Asked: Analyst call : Best practice in the healthcare org to secure MS copilot. Concerned about Hipaa compliance, patient information leaking, etc. 
AI policy already in place.
Has created a DLP policy.
The challenge is implementing sensitivity labels. 
how have other healthcare orgs secured and put guard rails for their Copilot rollouts.
Analyst Name: Paulo Aresta
Discussion Summary: Background & Context
•	The organization is in healthcare/science, with ~250 employees and a 7-person IT team.
•	AI usage (specifically ChatGPT) began in marketing, raising compliance concerns due to PHI/PII and HIPAA requirements.
•	An AI governance committee and policy were established, listing approved platforms and usage guidelines.
•	Microsoft Copilot was selected as the approved AI tool, with web grounding disabled to prevent data from leaving the tenant.
Current Challenges
•	Users are frustrated by Copilot’s web grounding being disabled, limiting its usefulness to internal data.
•	The organization does not currently use sensitivity labels, and there are concerns about the effectiveness and complexity of implementing them.
•	DLP policies are being considered, but there is uncertainty about best practices, especially regarding user actions such as copy-paste and uploading files.
Risks & Threats Identified
•	Risks include users uploading or copy-pasting sensitive data into GenAI tools, and the potential for Copilot to crawl and expose sensitive data.
•	Even with DLP and sensitivity labels, there are gaps (e.g., downloaded files from external systems like Salesforce may not have labels).
•	User behavior and training are critical; technology alone cannot eliminate all risks.
Best Practice Recommendations
•	Layered Controls: Use sensitivity labels, DLP policies, and conditional access to create multiple guardrails.
•	Sensitivity Labels: 
o	Labels are foundational but must be combined with other controls for effectiveness.
o	Copilot can be configured to ignore data with certain labels.
o	Labels may not persist in all file types (e.g., PDFs), and enforcement can be inconsistent.
•	Custom DLP Policies: 
o	Supplement out-of-the-box identifiers with custom rules (e.g., project codes, keywords) to reduce false positives and cover more use cases.
o	Use DLP to block uploads or copy-paste of sensitive data, even if labels are missing.
•	Phased Implementation: 
o	Start with audit mode to monitor and fine-tune policies before enforcing blocks.
o	Roll out to controlled groups, gather feedback, and expand gradually.
•	User Training & Awareness: 
o	Mandatory training on AI and DLP policies is essential.
o	Ongoing communication and support to reduce user friction.
•	Supplementing Purview: 
o	Purview works best within Microsoft 365; coverage for external systems (e.g., Salesforce) is limited.
o	Consider third-party DLP tools if risk appetite or requirements demand broader coverage.
Project & Change Management
•	Implementing sensitivity labels is a significant undertaking, requiring careful planning, stakeholder engagement, and user training.
•	The AI governance committee can help define data classification and ownership.
•	Expect user friction and plan for change management.

What would you like to discuss?
- gotchas
- transition team will need some ways to overcome resistance
- set up, sure, but what about the buy-in

Went over the internal construction of how MS Fabric is being designed and the product enhanced.
- consider the end-user parts of the platform such as PowerBI and the OpenAI interfaces (which can be used with any AI solution) as "one large pile or stack of metadata". This describes who is using what data via its own semantic layer that allows access to built to underlying data assets. For those within Azure/MS Fabric, there is higher compatibility for combining metadata with the data management tier, but it is likely at best 60-65% auto-matching, with little inference for the unresolved content.
- the data management layers have their own "pile of metadata" that tracks pipelines through Factory, Azure Warehouse, OneLake, etc. If the in/out data descriptions match up across these components, the combinations are much more efficient than the user/interfacing stack, higher even than 90% at times.
- Purview has the job of putting things together. When they automatch (think 65% times 90% as best case, or about 61%, and as low as 45%) then Purview succeeds and the metadata, lineage, stats and more can all match. But your services contractor will need to assist your team and get assistance from your SMEs to match the outliers and this is required for Purview to start accelerating the solution delivery and enable the mid- and advanced levels of data management self-service.

Date: 6-Feb-26
Reference Number: 19272742
Buyer Region: NORTHAM
Buyer Industry: Healthcare
Buyer Role: IT/Infrastructure and Operations - Other Role
Purpose: Written Response - Agentic AI Security
Question Asked: Automation is a major priority, with emphasis on leveraging agentic AI functionalities per directive from the CIO.  Darrell is interested in understanding the security implications of Agentic AI as the landscape evolves. He seeks insights to address concerns specific to the payer world, where additional regulatory considerations are present. What are the security risks associated with Agentic AI? How can we ensure compliance with industry-specific regulations while implementing Agentic AI solutions?
Analyst Name: Mary Ruddy
Discussion Summary: This is regarding your request for a written response about Agentic AI Security.

Thank you for your request. Agentic AI Security is a fast-moving area that is still developing and is not yet mature.  For a high level perspective, see How to Secure Enterprise Agentic AI Ambition. Note also that shadow AI is now an issue for organizations. See OpenClaw (Formerly Moltbot, Clawdbot): Agentic Productivity Comes With Unacceptable Cybersecurity Risk.

There are multiple aspects to consider depending on whether Molina Health is thinking about:
•	Securing AI agents Molina Healthcare develops itself. See for example:
o	How to Secure Custom-Built AI Agents 
•	Securing use of AI agents developed by others. For example, Anthropic released Claude for Healthcare: https://www.anthropic.com/news/healthcare-life-sciences. You may also be interested in the recently published: With Opus 4.6, Anthropic Sets Its Sights on Automating Business Workflows. It provides perspective on the process automation that Anthropic is developing. Note, it is still very early days for sensitive use cases. Anthropic is describing it as HIPAA-ready, but as with any tool, actual HIPAA compliance depends on how a tool is used. For additional perspective see also:
o	How to Securely Delegate Access From Humans to AI Agents 
o	IAM for LLM-Based AI Agents

Other general security recommendations
•	Don’t blindly trust third-party agentic services. Evaluate them from a security perspective and secure your usage of them with strong identity, access, and monitoring controls.
•	The security of multi-agent processes is still nascent.
•	Most actual value is from using agents to augment, rather than replace, human work.

Areas of particular concern to payers include:
•	Compliance with HIPAA, which implies that data also must be secure. 
•	Data security and leakage when using LLMs and agentic AI is a significant concern.
•	If you are processing payments by credit card, there is also the need to be PCI compliant. There have been announcements about infrastructure for agentic commerce, but it is still early days. See How to Securely Experiment with OpenAI and Google Agentic Commerce.
•	Depending on what services you are coordinating, you may also find AI Weaponization in Biotechnology Is a Cybersecurity Problem relevant.

One of the challenges of written responses, is that it is a one way, rather than bi-directional form of communication. The potential use of AI for payer use cases is a very sensitive area. If you have additional questions, or wish to discuss areas covered in this response, please request an inquiry.

Date: 28-Jan-26
Reference Number: 19224155
Buyer Region: EMEA
Buyer Industry: Banking, Finance and Insurance
Buyer Role: Finance Transformation
Purpose: Chapter Lead Role
Question Asked: UPDATE: 1/12
From the inquiry that we got from the client, it seems that he wants to have a high level conversation around the role of Chapter Lead within an agile organization. They want to discuss how other organizations use this positions, what best practices there are, what has worked well and maybe not so well for others to learn from those mistakes.
 
Using Gartner's Private GPT Chat, a chapter lead is defined as follows:
 
A Chapter Lead in an IT organization is a leadership role commonly found in companies that use agile frameworks, especially the Spotify model or similar scaled agile setups.
Key aspects of a Chapter Lead role:
 
- People Management: The Chapter Lead is responsible for the professional development, coaching, and performance management of a group of employees (the "chapter") who share similar skills or job functions (e.g., software developers, QA engineers, UX designers).
- Community of Practice: Chapters are communities of practice across different agile teams (called "squads" in the Spotify model). The Chapter Lead ensures that best practices, standards, and knowledge are shared within the chapter.
- Dual Role: Often, Chapter Leads also work as individual contributors within a squad, so they balance hands-on technical work with leadership duties.
- Skill Development: They focus on upskilling, mentoring, and supporting chapter members in their career growth.
- Alignment: Chapter Leads help ensure consistency in ways of working, tools, and methodologies across squads.
 
In summary:
A Chapter Lead is a people manager and technical mentor for a group of specialists within an IT organization, fostering skill development, sharing best practices, and ensuring alignment across teams. 
 
The term "Chapter Lead" has been popularized by Spotify, see below doc for reference, maybe the authors of the research could have further insights into the topic:
https://www.gartner.com/document-reader/document/4092199?ref=askgartner-smart
Analyst Name: Dave Micko
Discussion Summary: Key ask:
To understand how to refine the chapter lead role by gaining insights into best practices, recent trends, and necessary capabilities from leading organizations.

Summary:
- The discussion focused on refining the chapter lead role within the organization, with the customer seeking insights on best practices and recent trends in similar roles across leading organizations.
- The customer inquired about the challenges faced with the chapter lead role, particularly around accountability, role clarity, and career path progression.
- The Gartner expert indicated that many organizations have moved away from the Spotify model and the concept of chapter leads, suggesting a shift towards more traditional roles like engineering managers and individual contributors.
- The expert explained that successful organizations often separate people leadership from operational leadership, emphasizing the importance of autonomous teams for work delivery.
- The customer expressed interest in understanding how engineering managers align with product managers and the interaction between leads and managers.
- The expert elaborated on the engineering manager role, highlighting that it typically involves people management rather than coding, with a focus on mentoring junior and mid-career professionals.
- The discussion touched on the typical team size for engineering managers, with the expert noting that it varies but often ranges from 8 to 10, influenced by organizational needs and AI advancements.
- The customer asked about career progression, and the expert explained that engineering managers often emerge from engineering backgrounds but should not be forced into management roles solely for career advancement.
- The expert shared insights on organizational complexity and how it influences the seniority of engineering managers, suggesting that complexity often dictates the level of engineering management needed.
- The conversation covered global versus local role profiles, with the expert recommending a core global profile that can be locally extended to maintain consistency while allowing for regional variations.
- The session concluded with the expert offering to provide additional resources and materials to help the customer further explore organizational structures and role definitions.

Date: 5-Mar-26
Reference Number: 19370017
Buyer Region: NORTHAM
Buyer Industry: Government
Buyer Role: CISO or Head of Information Security
Purpose: Healthcare AI Risks
Question Asked: Follow up our call 19340056 with Allison Dennis
Allison is going to review our presentation and provide her feedback and resources to help us complete our presentation
Analyst Name: Allison Dennis
Discussion Summary: 1. Main Focus of the Discussion

The board is seeking a clear understanding of the main risks introduced by AI, especially in healthcare settings.
The priority is to identify the most significant risks, understand their impact on hospitals, and discuss possible mitigation strategies.
There is a particular interest in both general AI risks and those specific to healthcare, with an emphasis on practical, high-level recommendations rather than exhaustive detail.

2. Major AI Risks for Healthcare Organizations

Clinical Decision Support Risks: AI tools may misdiagnose or provide inaccurate recommendations. Strict governance and validation are required before deployment, including ongoing monitoring and human oversight.
Patient-Facing Chatbots: Patients using external AI tools (like ChatGPT) may receive incorrect medical advice, leading to misunderstandings when they interact with healthcare providers. Hospitals need to prepare to re-educate patients and control the information shared via patient portals.
Shadow AI: Clinicians or staff may use unapproved AI tools (e.g., copying sensitive data into public AI platforms), creating privacy and compliance risks. Policies, education, and technical controls are needed to address this.
Legal and Regulatory Risks: Laws and regulations around AI are evolving quickly. Healthcare organizations must maintain an up-to-date catalog of all AI systems in use, their purposes, and their compliance status to respond rapidly to regulatory changes.
Cybersecurity Risks: While not covered in detail, it was acknowledged that AI introduces new cybersecurity vulnerabilities, especially concerning patient data.

3. Mitigation Strategies

Governance: Implement strong oversight on AI tool selection, validation, and monitoring. Maintain a catalog of all AI systems and use cases.
Policy & Education: Develop clear acceptable use policies for clinicians and staff regarding AI tools. Provide ongoing training about risks, appropriate use, and potential biases.
Technical Controls: Block or monitor access to unapproved AI tools within the hospital network. Offer approved alternatives to meet clinicians’ needs.
Community of Practice: Encourage clinicians to share positive use cases and learn from each other, helping to spot misuse and foster responsible adoption.
Post-Deployment Monitoring: Continuously assess AI tools for accuracy, compliance, and relevance, especially after regulatory changes.

4. Implementation Considerations

The timeline for implementing these recommendations depends on the organization’s current state. Steps include establishing governance, creating policies, training, and deploying technical controls.
Some organizations may need to form new groups or assign new roles, but the key is to ensure all relevant perspectives (clinical, technical, legal, etc.) are considered.
Most healthcare organizations are still developing their AI governance frameworks; having a catalog of AI use cases is an ideal goal.

Date: 6-Mar-26
Reference Number: 19378451
Buyer Region: NORTHAM
Buyer Industry: Government
Buyer Role: CISO or Head of Information Security
Purpose: Healthcare AI Risks Updated Slides - Written Response
Question Asked: Follow up our call 19340056 with Allison Dennis
Allison is going to review our presentation and provide her feedback and resources to help us complete our presentation
Analyst Name: Allison Dennis
Discussion Summary: 1. Main Focus of the Discussion

The board is seeking a clear understanding of the main risks introduced by AI, especially in healthcare settings.
The priority is to identify the most significant risks, understand their impact on hospitals, and discuss possible mitigation strategies.
There is a particular interest in both general AI risks and those specific to healthcare, with an emphasis on practical, high-level recommendations rather than exhaustive detail.

2. Major AI Risks for Healthcare Organizations

Clinical Decision Support Risks: AI tools may misdiagnose or provide inaccurate recommendations. Strict governance and validation are required before deployment, including ongoing monitoring and human oversight.
Patient-Facing Chatbots: Patients using external AI tools (like ChatGPT) may receive incorrect medical advice, leading to misunderstandings when they interact with healthcare providers. Hospitals need to prepare to re-educate patients and control the information shared via patient portals.
Shadow AI: Clinicians or staff may use unapproved AI tools (e.g., copying sensitive data into public AI platforms), creating privacy and compliance risks. Policies, education, and technical controls are needed to address this.
Legal and Regulatory Risks: Laws and regulations around AI are evolving quickly. Healthcare organizations must maintain an up-to-date catalog of all AI systems in use, their purposes, and their compliance status to respond rapidly to regulatory changes.
Cybersecurity Risks: While not covered in detail, it was acknowledged that AI introduces new cybersecurity vulnerabilities, especially concerning patient data.

3. Mitigation Strategies

Governance: Implement strong oversight on AI tool selection, validation, and monitoring. Maintain a catalog of all AI systems and use cases.
Policy & Education: Develop clear acceptable use policies for clinicians and staff regarding AI tools. Provide ongoing training about risks, appropriate use, and potential biases.
Technical Controls: Block or monitor access to unapproved AI tools within the hospital network. Offer approved alternatives to meet clinicians’ needs.
Community of Practice: Encourage clinicians to share positive use cases and learn from each other, helping to spot misuse and foster responsible adoption.
Post-Deployment Monitoring: Continuously assess AI tools for accuracy, compliance, and relevance, especially after regulatory changes.

4. Implementation Considerations

The timeline for implementing these recommendations depends on the organization’s current state. Steps include establishing governance, creating policies, training, and deploying technical controls.
Some organizations may need to form new groups or assign new roles, but the key is to ensure all relevant perspectives (clinical, technical, legal, etc.) are considered.
Most healthcare organizations are still developing their AI governance frameworks; having a catalog of AI use cases is an ideal goal.

Date: 3-Mar-26
Reference Number: 19343491
Buyer Region: ASIAPAC
Buyer Industry: Energy and Utilities
Buyer Role: IT - Other Role
Purpose: MS Power Platform
Question Asked: I'm specifically after some information regarding MS Power Platform - Two parts 
- Where it fits in a corporate ecosystem - what is it good for/bad for and be wary. 
and how it fits with over MS offerings such as dataverse and other database options

“What are the primary use cases, strengths, and weaknesses of Microsoft Power Platform in a corporate ecosystem?”
“What cautions, governance requirements, and risk considerations should organizations consider when adopting Microsoft Power Platform?”
Analyst Name: Mike Warren
Discussion Summary: Contact did not join the call. I answered the questions and sent a follow up response via email with the following key take aways.

1) What are the primary use cases, strengths, and weaknesses of Microsoft Power Platform in a corporate ecosystem?
Microsoft Power Platform is primarily a platform to empower Low Code/No Code developers.
The main personas supported would be Citizen Developers (Business power users), IT Governance teams, BI teams, and Professional Developers

Strengths
Its primary strength is in extending existing business processes.
As a low code platform it is also very strong in provide speed to value enabling rapid solution delivery
If you are a Microsoft shop it provides native access and integration with Microsoft's ecosystem including Teams, M365, Dynamics CRM, and Entra Security
Power Automate offers solid RPA and API Hybrid capabilities
It also has native integration to AI features such as CoPilot, AI Builder, and Custom GPT integration
Governance capabilities are available to support the platform (DLP, ALM, Segregated Environments)

    Weaknesses
This platform is not designed to support large systems or heavy transactional usage. 
There are hard caps on size and performance will quickly degrade under heavy transactional load
Licensing can become complex with deployment and usage of the environment leading to differing pricing (Per app vs per user, Premium connectors, RPA licensing, and Dataverse capacity).
ALM and DevOps can be problematic in their software promotion 

Power Platform is the right tool when you are 
Needing workflow automation with human approvals
Need a lightweight operational app
Need a frontend UX over an API
Need to embed an app into Teams

Power Platform is not the right tool when you are
Building mission critical, high throughput applications
Building large scale, high performance applications
Building large scale data warehouse or BI

2) What cautions or governance/risk considerations should organizations consider when adopting Microsoft Power Platform?
This is primarily a citizen developer platform, as such if ungoverned it could lead to sprawl and shadow IT operations
Governance is an absolute must from the start, and luckily there are some built in capabilities that you will need to deploy and configure.
To properly secure the environment you will need to start by creating an Environment Strategy. Power Platform allows you to create individual environments. Access controls are configured at the environment level.
Implement Data Loss Prevention (DLP). DLP will allow you to control which connectors are available and how they can interact with the ecosystem.
Ensure Dataverse creation is controlled via policy. This will prevent data leakage and limit the types of work that can be done to those with proper trust and need.
Implement Application Lifecycle Management (ALM). This will ensure version control of application, proper environment promotion, and dependency management.
Implement licensing and cost monitoring through Power BI. You can establish monitors to track premium connector usage, Dataverse storage, automation execution volumes, and RPA bots. These will be your biggest cost drivers.
Application monitoring and discovery through Power BI. Power Platform allows internal connectors which let you monitor app inventory, flow inventory, Dataverse tables, API usage, and Owner activity.
Establish lifecycle policies for Apps. Every app should have an owner, business sponsor, and lifecycle classification. Policies can be established to alert when governance conditions are met.

3) How does Microsoft Dataverse integrate with Microsoft Power Platform and how does Dataverse compare to other Microsoft database options (such as Azure SQL Database, SharePoint lists, and Dataverse for Teams) for use with Power Platform?
Dataverse is a lite transactional database specifically designed for Power Plat

**Background:**  
In building our data infrastructure, our company assumes the use of multiple generative AI platforms (multi-platform), such as Copilot, Gemini, and ChatGPT. As a basic policy, we envision consolidating all data into a new data infrastructure, ensuring that high-quality data is stored in a single location. On the other hand, when AI retrieves data from the data infrastructure for graphing or data processing, we assume that such processing will be performed on the AI platform side (e.g., Copilot, Gemini, Azure, etc.). If the AI platform, such as Gemini, has its own database functionality (e.g., BigQuery), there is an opinion that, for more efficient data utilization by AI, it may be best to copy and store AI-ready data in each platform’s database as needed. However, if we adopt this approach, despite our intention to unify the data infrastructure, we would end up having to maintain the same data in multiple locations for each AI platform, which raises concerns from a data governance perspective.

**Questions:**
1. In a multi-AI platform environment, should AI-ready data be consolidated only in a centralized data infrastructure, or is it optimal to replicate and place the same data in each AI platform’s database to maximize the efficiency of each platform?
2. When data processing is performed on the AI platform side, what kind of integration architecture (method of connecting data) is recommended to optimally handle data from the central data infrastructure?

Purpose: Sierra AI

UPDATE:
Client leads Innovation at Prudential so is senior. She has a renewal in June and they are interested in SierraAI. 

Overall technical assessment of SierraAI- strengths and weaknesses
Comparable products in the market (particularly in the call center space)
How SierraAI is being utilized across the FI industry
Case studies, benchmarks for ROIs, successes, learnings, etc.
Analyst Name: Francesco Vicchi
Discussion Summary: As you approach your Salesforce contract renewal, evaluating the differences of traditional CRM-bound AI with agentic platforms is a strategic priority.
Below is a summary of our discussion, focusing on the comparison between Sierra AI and Salesforce Agentforce, as well as integration considerations for your Genesys environment.

Executive Summary: Sierra AI vs. Salesforce Agentforce
The core distinction lies in architecture vs. ecosystem. While Salesforce is building AI into the CRM you already own, Sierra AI is an "AI-native" platform designed to orchestrate complex, autonomous conversations across your entire stack.
Sierra AI Overview
To define it simply, Sierra AI can be associated to a Conversational AI Platforms at its surface, but it is built with a fundamentally agentic core. While traditional chatbots follow rigid scripts or simple decision trees, Sierra uses an orchestrator to reason through tasks. It doesn't just talk; it acts as an agent that can navigate complex workflows autonomously to solve a customer's problem from start to finish. Sierra positions itself as a "constellation" of AI models. It does not rely on a single LLM but uses an orchestrator to select the best model (OpenAI, Anthropic, etc.) for a specific task.
•	Agentic Orchestration: Unlike simple bots, Sierra agents are designed to reason and execute multi-step tasks autonomously.
•	Pricing Model: Sierra typically uses an outcome-based pricing model. You pay for "successful resolutions" rather than per-seat or per-token. While this aligns cost to value, it requires a very precise definition of "success" to avoid billing friction.
•	Voice Excellence: Sierra is often cited for its high-quality, real-time voice interactions, though latency (the "awkward pause") remains a critical metric to test during a possible POC.

Salesforce Agentforce Overview
Agentforce is the evolution of Einstein, deeply embedded in the Salesforce Data Cloud.
•	Native Integration: The primary advantage is "zero integration." It has immediate access to your Salesforce objects, permissions, and historical data.
•	Pricing Model: Typically consumption-based, which can be easier to predict if you have high volume but lower complexity.
•	User Interface: It uses a low-code "Agent Builder," making it more accessible for your existing Salesforce admins to manage without heavy developer intervention.
________________________________________
Integration with Genesys and Salesforce
A key concern was how these tools play with your existing Genesys contact center infrastructure.
•	Genesys + Salesforce: These two have a robust, pre-integrated relationship (CX Cloud). If you stay within this ecosystem, the "plumbing" is already handled.
•	Sierra + Genesys/Salesforce: Sierra is built to be a layer above these systems. It uses APIs and connector to pull context from Salesforce and can route calls to or from Genesys. You gain flexibility and a "best-of-breed" AI, but you inherit an additional integration maintenance.
________________________________________

Next Steps
Here below some noted where Gartner analyzes Sierra and the case study we discussed about:

•	Cool Vendors in Agentic AI 

•	2025 Cool Vendors in CRM 

•	Case Study: GenAI IVR Reinvents the Voice Experience 

If you need any assistance with this topic, please don't hesitate to reach out to me or my colleagues.

Analyst Name: Jeroen Cornelissen
Discussion Summary: Summary:
- Erwin Vreeman described his role as an analyst at BasicFit since April, with prior experience at the Vrije Universiteit and Atos, and explained that BasicFit is exploring AI governance due to growing internal AI usage and risks without clear guardrails.
- Jeroen described the draft governance document as notably strong compared to typical policy documents and recommended clarifying distinctions between use cases like Everyday AI versus game-changing AI that require full data-capability readiness.
- Erwin highlighted challenges in member retention and suggested AI-driven insights, such as personalized membership offers or chatbot handling of subscription queries, could help extend customer lifetime value.
- Jeroen advised visualizing the governance framework with a clear process flow and lines of defense linking roles, reporting, and responsibilities, and recommended using a “praatplaat” overview to improve readability and stakeholder understanding.
- Erwin requested guidance on next steps, practical implementation, and balancing high-level AI strategy with detailed governance, noting the need for both strategy definition and operational guardrail documents.
- Jeroen outlined a tactical approach: first establish concrete AI guardrails specifying acceptable behavior (e.g., data usage, personal vs. corporate AI tools), then roll out targeted training or micro-learning modules before activating corporate licenses for tools like ChatGPT or Copilot.
- Jeroen emphasized adopting a risk-based framework that differentiates everyday AI tools employees bring in, corporate-licensed AI features, and bespoke AI in customer-facing applications, and suggested two concrete use cases be tested against the framework to validate applicability.
- Erwin wondered how to ensure document uptake and accountability; Jeroen recommended combining a full policy for audit purposes with role-based extracts or e-sign acknowledgment, plus operational committees or Community of Practice to oversee implementation and monitoring.
- Jeroen noted that compliance with EU AI Act already positions BasicFit well for UK requirements, and stressed that future game-changing AI will demand strong data quality, metadata management, and clearly defined steward and deployer roles.
- Erwin confirmed he will share the document with their board and schedule governance council meetings, monitoring procedures, and practical “rubber hits the road” discussions, and welcomed Jeroen’s offer for a second review.

Key Takeaways:
- A strong initial draft governance policy can be enhanced by distinguishing everyday versus transformative AI use and aligning each with data readiness requirements.
- Visual frameworks illustrating lines of defense, reporting relationships, and process flows improve stakeholder engagement and clarify accountability.
- Start with clear, tactical guardrails and simple training modules before enabling corporate AI tools; use risk-based categorization of AI use cases.
- Consider testing the framework on two representative use cases (e.g., FitBuddy features and subscription-support chatbot) to ensure practicality and refine governance.
- Combine a comprehensive policy for audit and liability with role-specific summaries and acknowledgment processes to drive adoption.
- Establish an AI Governance Council or Community of Practice for ongoing oversight, monitoring, and periodic strategy updates.
- Leverage compliance with the EU AI Act to simplify UK alignment; prepare for future game-changing AI with robust data quality and stewardship practices.

GOAL: position the data platforms, and his reports will be answerable and responsible, but this is more exploratory and learning at this point. No specific goal to increase or decrease use of any of these platforms. What does Palantir have that other platforms lack?

Palantir’s core technology stack is composed of four primary, interoperable platforms:
Foundry: This is the central platform for data integration, management, and analytics. It ingests data into a proprietary database and handles application delivery by relying on three technical engines: continuous data profiling, machine learning over usage logs, and graph analytics.

Gotham: Operating as a metadata analytics and forensics tool, Gotham "pierces" the metadata within Foundry to analyze system operations. This allows engineers to optimize processes and "hunt" for new use cases within a client's data landscape.

AIP (Artificial Intelligence Platform): Rather than acting as a standalone AI or Large Language Model (LLM), AIP is an AI orchestration engine. It coordinates external, third-party AI models (like OpenAI, Claude, SageMaker, others) by passing them data and prompts from Foundry, and then integrates the AI-generated results back into the workflow.

Apollo: This is the underlying infrastructure layer that manages the continuous delivery, deployment, and physical resource allocation of the software across various cloud and on-premises environments.

Impact on Professional Services and Implementation Delivery:
Palantir operates on a "coterminous delivery" model, meaning its platforms are heavily bundled with mandatory, long-term professional services to ensure successful implementation. This approach shapes their delivery in several critical ways:

Dependence on Forward Deployed Engineers (FDEs): Palantir's primary market differentiator is not its technology—which is considered non-differentiating and replicable with lower-cost tools—but its elite "guru-level" data engineers. Because of this, the vast majority of Palantir implementations require a minimum of two FDEs continuously embedded over a three-year contract span
.
Intentional Metadata Opacity and "Stickiness": Palantir’s platforms are intentionally "metadata resistant" and feature opaque metadata behavior, meaning they do not easily share design and operational metadata outside the platform. During implementations, FDEs often do not link the metadata from the initial discovery phase into the production phase. As a result, the "institutional memory" of why specific integration decisions were made remains strictly with the Palantir engineers. This opacity creates a highly "sticky" environment that fosters extreme dependency on Palantir's staff, securing an ongoing annuity services revenue stream.

High Costs and the Knowledge Transfer Gap: Because of this services-heavy, closed-stack model, Palantir's pricing is typically 2.15 to 2.45 times higher than market benchmarks. To combat the "knowledge transfer gap" and avoid permanent lock-in, clients must aggressively manage their implementation contracts. It is recommended to budget an additional 150-165% during the first 6-9 months specifically to fund Palantir staff to train internal employees, mandate key personnel clauses, and hold back up to 20% of fees until knowledge transfer milestones are successfully met.

Possible assessment to devise remediation of the services model if desired?

Discussion Summary: The attached research goes further into depth on these features. I have answered the specific questions to narrow the focus. If there are further questions, please schedule an inquiry and we’d be happy to discuss. 

•	Extensibility & ITSM Integration 
o	Which vendors offer native or add-on ITSM integration for document workflows? 
The only direct party integration with ITSM is between Box and Service. And ServiceNow has built integrations into Box, Dropbox, Google Drive, and Microsoft SharePoint. There may be 3rd party solutions.

•	Regulated Environments & Data Sovereignty 
o	Which vendors are most mature for auditing, classification, and traceability? 
Most platforms have similar auditing and traceability aspects. Classification varies and has become more common through the use of GenAI. Most vendors use either Copilot, Gemini, or OpenAI

o	Who has limitations with on-premises or data sovereignty needs? 
Box, which was mentioned above, only offers a SaaS solution. It has limited data residency in that the documents are stored in local geographies, but properties are stored centrally. The more traditional platforms. Hyland, OpenText, SER, and others have primarily on premise solutions, which would provide the best data sovereignty. Two of these also offer SaaS-like solutions. SER is based in Germany and probably fits best from a sovereign aspect. OpenText, though Canadian, deploys on Google.

o	How does vendor suitability change if on-prem/hybrid deployment is required? 
Very few offer a truly integrated approaches between SaaS and on premises solutions. Those would be Egnyte and M-Files, possibly SER. Others like Hyland and OpenText would require their hosted platform for a hybrid scenario.

•	Hybrid Document Management 
o	Which platforms support both collaborative and regulated documentation in one instance? 
Since the focus is on defense, rather than highly regulated industry like pharmaceuticals. Many of these have options. If we look at U.S. FedRamp guidelines for high (defined as managing highly sensitive, unclassified government information) Box is the only vendor with this. If we look at ITAR (International Traffic in Arms Regulations) required by defense contractors, Box and Egnyte are the only vendors that currently state support. 

o	Which require separate instances or extra modules for high-regulation? 
This really depends on the requirements for “high-regulation.” If it’s security related, the above hopefully addresses this. If this is more about document processing, most vendors are similar.

o	Where do governance or traceability gaps typically occur in hybrid models? 
Probably the main gap is that the hybrid models are two separate instances that typically do not work as one. Meaning these are isolated silos that can have different users and different content. 

•	Metadata & Structure 
o	Who offers native metadata/taxonomy management and structured templates?
They all have metadata and taxonomy management but not to the level of what you would see in a data catalog tool for instance. 
 
o	Who relies on plug-ins, and how does this affect traceability and operations?
If this is about data catalogs, currently no vendor integrates with a data catalog. They can import data from other sources. Some may be able to synchronize the data live.

•	Enterprise Architecture Integration 
o	Which solutions integrate robustly with EA tools, and is this via a shared data model or basic connectors? 
Document management platforms usually don’t work with EA tools. If this is a requirement, then it may be better to focus on the document workflows with tools like Microsoft Power Platform or UI Path. UI Path supports Box, Dropbox, Google, and Microsoft SharePoint. Microsoft Power Platform supports those and a dozen other, of which Egnyte is mentioned above.

Additional Client Details:
Analyst Interaction #19264377- original .
Analyst Name: Mark A. Beyer
Discussion Summary: Shane will now be working with Tara, and has recent meetings with top brass and civilian counterparts. He is now the head of Digital Information lead.

His clients are really enthused with Palantir demonstrations the way that NAVAIR is in this combined configuration. One of the concepts he has to build upon is understanding how AI really works (he uses Gemini, OpenAI and is also looking at Antrhopic).

Explained about how chunking works in unstructured data.

Instead of populating a knowledge graph as a bespoke design based on business user or steward inputs, consider "discovering" the knowledge graph dynamically.

A dynamically constructed knowledge graph is built by combining taxonomy, ontology, contextual semantics (tagging), and system observability. It acts as a structured representation of knowledge that connects entities (nodes) through relationships (edges), serving as both an output and an enabler of graph analytics.
You will build out four core components:

Ontology (The Theoretical Intent): Ontology represents the top-down, desired design of business data structures, concepts, and relationships. It is an intentionally biased, theoretical model of how the business wants its data to be organized. Because ontology, models, and schemas are theoretical constructs, they remain unproven until they are populated with actual data.

Taxonomy (The Empirical Reality): Taxonomy represents the bottom-up, empirical reality of how data actually exists, behaves, and is utilized within a system. It provides the real-world data examples that either prove or challenge the theoretical design of the ontology.

Machine Learning (The Analysis Engine): Machine learning, used in conjunction with continuous data profiling and graph analytics, acts as the engine that discovers an organization's true taxonomy. ML analyzes the operational "exhaust" of systems—such as usage logs, queries, views, and processing code—to identify patterns, variations, and anomalies in how data is actually used. Crucially, machine learning and observability metadata provide "directedness" to the graph. This prevents the graph from running in infinite circles (a "no resolve" issue) by mathematically determining which pathways should be amplified or diminished based on actual business goals.

Tagging (The Contextual Interpreter): Tagging applies human vocabulary and business context to the nodes, edges, and hops within the knowledge graph. Instead of relying on manual input, these tags are often generated by "scraping" existing assets—such as report headers, dashboard titles, spreadsheet columns, and the surrounding prose or source citations found in unstructured documents. By attaching these real-world business terms as tags to the graph, the system dynamically resolves terminology conflicts like synonyms and homonyms.

The Result: A Three-Part Graph Ultimately, these elements combine to form a comprehensive, three-part knowledge graph: the machine-learning-observed utilization builds the taxonomy (the reality), the business's preferred ontology defines the intent, and the tagging provides a semantic resolution layer that acts as the interpreter sitting directly between them. Once this graph is developed and annotated with tags and utilization statistics, it can power rapid searches, prompt engineering, and GenAI capabilities.

Unstructured chunking is the process of breaking down unstructured content into smaller units or "chunks" that can be analyzed, tagged, and further processed by automated systems such as natural language processing (NLP) models and large language models (LLMs). Because unstructured data is inherently complex and free-form, chunking helps to impart a degree of structure that is essential for downstream processing. For example, when proce

Discussion Summary: 🧠 Detailed Key Discussion Points (Context, Why It Matters, Conclusions)
1. Microsoft EA renewals are no longer “simple volume plays”
What was discussed:
The client described how Microsoft EA negotiations have shifted from straightforward server/user counts to complex, multi‑SKU agreements that now include Copilot, security, data residency, fabric, and agent capabilities. The renewal is a mixed EA + SCA structure, reflecting changing usage patterns and declining traditional volumes.
Why it matters:
EA complexity increases the risk of:
	• Over‑buying future‑state licenses
	• Locking in assumptions (e.g., headcount or adoption) that may not materialize
	• Losing effective discount position versus prior agreements
Conclusion:
The renewal must be assessed relative to the client’s prior discount and entitlement baseline, not in isolation. Structural decisions matter as much as headline commercial terms.
2. Discount “optics” vs. real commercial position
What was discussed:
The client referenced an average discount range across SKUs, while the discussion clarified that Microsoft’s removal of historical A/B/C tier starting discounts means customers often perceive stability when, in reality, they may be net worse off than previous terms.
Why it matters:
A flat or similar discount at renewal can still represent a step backwards if legacy tier benefits have been removed. Looking only at today’s percentage obscures real value erosion.
Conclusion:
The renewal should be benchmarked against prior effective pricing, not Microsoft’s current list‑based framing.
3. Copilot adoption assumptions are the biggest risk lever
What was discussed:
The client outlined a proposed large‑scale Copilot deployment, while acknowledging internal modelling shows:
	• Initial adoption spikes
	• Significant drop‑off after early usage periods
	• No realistic path to sustained 100% utilisation
This aligned with Gartner‑observed patterns discussed during the call: high early engagement followed by material decline.
Why it matters:
If Copilot quantities are fixed at peak assumptions:
	• The organisation risks paying for long‑term shelfware
	• ROI models may not hold beyond early enthusiasm
	• Workforce reductions (from productivity gains) directly undermine licensed user counts
Conclusion:
Copilot deployment should be phased, adjustable, or contractually flexible, rather than locked to an optimistic steady‑state assumption.
4. Bundling strategy (E5 / Copilot / Intune / future E‑levels)
What was discussed:
The conversation explored how Microsoft’s evolving bundles (including emerging “E‑level” constructs) may make bundled purchases more efficient than standalone add‑ons, particularly when multiple premium components are already in place.
Why it matters:
Incorrect bundling can:
	• Inflate long‑term cost
	• Limit flexibility as Microsoft introduces new SKUs
	• Create hidden duplication across security and AI tooling
Conclusion:
Bundle design must be forward‑looking, not just compliant with today’s SKU catalogue.
5. Open Licensing (OLS) inside the EA needs validation
What was discussed:
A significant portion of spend sits in open licensing constructs framed inside the EA, which raised concerns about program suitability and structural correctness.
Why it matters:
OLS is historically a different program with different optimisation levers. If mis‑structured:
	• The client may be paying more than necessary
	• Spend may be sitting in a sub‑optimal commercial vehicle
Conclusion:
A product‑level review (without pricing) is required to validate whether OLS placement is appropriate or whether alternative structures would reduce long‑term cost and complexity.
6. AI tool sprawl and cost control
What was discussed:
The client highlighted concurrent investment in multiple AI platforms (Microsoft Copilot, Claude, OpenAI, others), acknowledging that a single‑vendor AI strategy is unreal

Date: 6-Feb-26
Reference Number: 19270259
Buyer Region: LATINAM
Buyer Industry: Government
Buyer Role: Head of Enterprise Architecture
Purpose: Best Practices for Storytelling and Structuring Presentations
Question Asked: The client seeks guidance on effective storytelling and formatting techniques for presentations. There is no specific project in progress; rather, the goal is to enhance general presentation skills. The discussion will focus on frameworks, design principles, and narrative strategies that can be applied broadly. The client is interested in actionable tips and industry best practices. The session aims to build foundational knowledge for future presentation needs.
Analyst Name: Luis Mangi
Discussion Summary: For innovation leaders like yourself at Banco do Nordeste, mastering best practices in storytelling and presentation structuring is essential for engaging diverse audiences, communicating complex ideas, and driving organizational change. The first step in building an effective narrative is to map the audience profile: identify who the participants are, their levels of knowledge on the subject, their expectations, and any potential resistance. For example, when presenting a digital transformation proposal to traditional managers, it is important to anticipate questions related to security, costs, and operational impact, addressing these points clearly and strategically.

The ideal structure for a presentation begins with contextualizing the problem or opportunity, followed by presenting solutions, always connecting arguments to the audience’s reality. The “Situation, Complication, Resolution” framework is widely used by global consultancies because it guides the audience through a logical and engaging journey. Imagine you need to present a new open innovation program: start by showing data about the competitive landscape in the banking sector, highlight specific challenges faced by Banco do Nordeste, and then demonstrate how the proposed initiative can generate value, citing examples of banks that have achieved positive results with similar strategies.

The use of visual resources should be guided by the principle of simplicity. Clean slides, with little text and relevant images, make comprehension easier and avoid distractions. Design tools such as Canva or Beautiful.ai can help in creating professional presentations, even without advanced graphic design experience. Additionally, GenAI solutions like ChatGPT or Gamma allow you to generate scripts, summaries, and even customized narrative structure suggestions for different audiences, optimizing preparation time and ensuring greater accuracy in communication.

Another extremely useful resource is Google NotebookLM, a generative artificial intelligence tool developed to support the organization and analysis of information. NotebookLM allows you to bring together documents, notes, previous presentations, and research in a single environment, making it easier to quickly consult and build well-founded narratives. In the context of innovation leadership, NotebookLM can be used to map user journeys, structure innovative project proposals, or record collective insights during workshops and strategic meetings. For example, when preparing a presentation on open banking trends for Banco do Nordeste, you can use NotebookLM to gather references, build data-driven arguments, and generate different versions of your script, optimizing preparation time and ensuring alignment with the audience’s expectations. Furthermore, NotebookLM’s integration with other GenAI tools enhances the automation of tasks such as text summarization, generating impactful titles, and language review, allowing you to focus on strategy and message personalization.

From a narrative perspective, it is essential to build a story that provokes empathy and emotional connection. Share real success or failure stories, preferably related to the regional context or the financial sector, to make the content more relatable to the audience. For example, describe how a northeastern fintech managed to scale its operations thanks to innovative partnerships, highlighting the role of leadership and organizational culture.

Finally, always close your presentation by reinforcing the main takeaways and indicating clear next steps, such as invitations to collaborate, pilot tests, or in-depth discussions. Storytelling, combined with a logical structure and well-planned visual resources, transforms presentations into powerful tools for influence and mobilization, especially in strategic areas such as innovation.

Reference Number: 19219938
Buyer Region: EMEA
Buyer Industry: Manufacturing
Buyer Role: Enterprise Architecture - Other Role
Purpose: AI policy review - WR
Question Asked: I’m interested to understand if the document addresses the key aspects of compliance with the EU AI Act and whether it covers a range of domains from research & development to general AI usage for productivity gains and value creation.  The purpose of the policy was to support and encourage the safe and responsible use of AI in the workplace which covers:
•	General use of AI in the workplace
•	Use of AI as part of 3rd party services, platforms, and technologies
•	Developing & implementing AI solutions

I would welcome a written review of the policy and maybe followed by a call to discuss any key findings.
Analyst Name: Lauren Kornutick
Discussion Summary: Thank you for the opportunity to review your AI policy. I have reviewed and included a summary of my feedback below. I cannot comment on whether the policy is legally defensible for compliance purposes. That should be addressed with your in-house or external council.  However, I work with many of our clients on their AI policy and the EU AI Act and can provide general feedback and guidance on best practices/
 
I usually like to discuss policies because I often lack organizational context, so I would be happy to follow up on an inquiry if you have questions.  Overall, your principles are well drafted and contain the common set values for responsible AI use. This is also a very comprehensive policy. If the purpose of the policy is documentation for governance, it is well done. I have left some suggestions for improvement below to make it more consumable for employees:
 
A lot of the time we see clients shorten documents to give employees the information they need in a consumable way - they call these guidelines or department specific policy,etc. Tool: Policy Template for Acceptable Use of Generative AI is an example of something employee facing that is easy to consume. It’s a lot of great information, there is not anything missing, but it may be more easily consumed in training. 
 
I recommend that you bring what you want all employees to do forward. For example, consider reframing section 5 and 6 to acceptable use of AI and simplify:   
Honda personnel must not use public facing GenAI tools for work (e.g., ChatGPT, Gemini, etc.). Instead use x, y, z 
Honda personnel must review all GenAI output for x, y, z. 
Existing Confidentially and Information Security policy applies. Etc. 
Under section 6.3, the first sentence is likely sufficient as a policy statement. A lot of the underlying content can be used in training
Pull up “AI summarization can be used in teams but only with consent” 
 
Most employees don’t know what intellectual property and copyright are. My recommendation is to simplify to just “generated content needs to be verified” and provide IP training
 
How will an employee check for bias? Something like that may be better delegated to security and AI teams. 
 
Great description of high risk systems. I recommend adding a table with your risk categories and examples just so it pulls the point up. I usually see this addressed in a governance charter, not the general policy Getting Ready for the EU AI Act, Phase 4: Address Deployment Obligations for High-Risk AI Systems 
 
You’ve pulled in good points on developing AI. More mature organizations may provide developers with specific procedures about designing policy as code. Model Risk Control Guide for AI Governance and Toolkit: AI Controls for Governance of Models, Frameworks and Agents
 
For working with vendors, you already have great examples but consider adding a vendor attestation requirement. See  Getting Ready for the EU AI Act, Phase 3: Manage Third-Party Risks for an example
 
As part of our ongoing business and technology insights, Gartner is pleased to elaborate on your inquiry with this written response, and to provide further assistance as you progress with your work.

Date: 30-Jan-26
Reference Number: 19348587
Buyer Region: EMEA
Buyer Industry: Transportation
Buyer Role: Divisional Technology Leader
Purpose: Back Credit for Follow Up Written Response: AI Impact on Workforce
Question Asked: the impact of AI / Gen AI / Agentic AI on our workforce, first the IT Workforce but also the impact on Business Workforce. I want to discuss impact on type of activities, number of resources, and how to manage AI Agents as a new element to our workforce. 

I would like to request Mary Mesaglio for this HVE.
Analyst Name: Tori Paulman
Discussion Summary: OCIO’s question: “Scenario’s of the impact of AI / Gen AI / Agentic AI on our workforce, first the IT Workforce but also the impact on Business Workforce. I want to discuss impact on type of activities, number of resources, and how to manage AI Agents as a new element to our workforce”

I have broken down the question and highlighted where to find the answer in the attached presentation file and included supporting research. Please accept my apologies for not making the answer clear enough during our time together.
AI differentiation: It is important to differentiation between GenAI and traditional AI approaches, and agentic AI when talking about the impact on the workforce. (slides 107-111)
•	AI: General Artificial Intelligence (automation, machine learning, etc.).
•	Gen AI: Generative AI (tools like ChatGPT, DALL-E, etc.).
•	Agentic AI: AI agents that can act autonomously, make decisions, and perform tasks.
Two Workforce Groups: The journey for the IT worker has many parallels to the journey of the non-IT worker. CIOs who lead this evolution by anticipating workforce evolution within IT can set a precedent and establish a framework that other business units can follow, amplifying their impact across the entire workforce. (Slide 64) 
 
IT tasks that will change near-term:
•	Coding / software engineering: Routine, undifferentiated coding and standard integration work will be increasingly handled by AI tools and agents; engineers will shift toward orchestrating, configuring and supervising AI that generates, tests and deploys code.
•	Development workflows and CI/CD: AI pair‑programming, AI engineering toolchains and embedding GenAI into the SDLC will change how code is produced, tested and promoted to production.
•	Troubleshooting and incident response: Agentic AI and GenAI will be used to correlate telemetry, spot anomalies, isolate root causes and even take remedial steps in IT operations, enabling semiautonomous or autonomous incident handling.
•	System maintenance / Day‑2 operations: GenAI and agents will assist with routine Day‑2 tasks (monitoring, configuration updates, self‑healing suggestions), and in networking specifically will accelerate configuration, vendor translation and troubleshooting.
•	Observability, security and governance tasks: New controls (AI gateways, guardrails, logging, canary testing) and pipeline hardening will be required to secure models, RAG inputs and engineering pipelines.
Business tasks that will change near-term:
•	Data analysis and decision support: Business consumers will prefer intelligent assistants and embedded analytics over dashboards for many decisions; GenAI/agents will deliver proactive, contextual insights and embed into workflows.
•	Customer service and contact centers: GenAI and agentic AI will automate routine customer interactions, generate/summarize content, power chatbots/AI agents and reduce human transfers, changing contact‑center and digital‑service workflows.
•	Process‑centric business functions (HR, supply chain, finance): Agentic AI will execute or semi-autonomously support process steps (e.g., order approvals, talent acquisition workflows, supply‑chain decisions), reinventing process analysis and reducing some specialized manual tasks.
•	Product development, simulation and market modeling: Intelligent simulation and domain‑specialized models will reshape product R&D and market planning; agents will enable scenario optimization and faster design cycles.
Getting started with your Human-AI workforce journey: The Human-AI Workforce Journey: 5 Steps for CIOs to Accelerate AI-Readiness  (Slides 5 – 22)
•	Adopt scenario planning and role redesign: Use scenario workshops to identify which tasks will be automated, augmented or newly created; redefine roles toward creativity, critical thinking and AI orchestration.
•	Upskill and create AI‑literate teams: Invest in AI literacy, analytics engineering, AI secu

Date: 17-Feb-26
Reference Number: 19263833
Buyer Region: NORTHAM
Buyer Industry: Government
Buyer Role: Engineering
Purpose: Understanding Palantir’s Role in Document-Centric Digital Thread Initiatives
Question Asked: Purpose:
Our organization is exploring Palantir as a potential solution to support our digital thread initiatives. We are highly document-centric, relying on electronic documents and correspondence as our primary sources of truth rather than structured databases. We seek objective, third-party insights into Palantir’s capabilities and limitations, especially regarding document-centric environments and digital thread use cases.

Questions:
- How is Palantir typically used to enable digital thread initiatives, particularly in organizations that are document-centric rather than database-driven?
- Does Palantir offer robust capabilities for extracting, organizing, and leveraging data from electronic documents and correspondence, or is its focus primarily on database-centric data extraction?
- How does Palantir complement or integrate with existing document management tools (such as Sendia) within broader digital thread strategies?
Analyst Name: Mark A. Beyer
Discussion Summary: Palantir.net for digital content.
-ontology
- sentiment
- semantic
- context
Naval nuclear labs is propulsion for nuclear powered naval vessels (carriers, submarines, others). Significant file and document basis. Working on parsers over documents to catalog the results. So as a result we are more file-centered regarding our assets. Broadly across the Navy, Palantir is being used, however in Dave's understanding, Palantir is about the use of data versus context in most cases. "Is there a place for the file-side of things?"

-	The primary point from out call is:
o	Palantir is primarily an information/data management platform with application processing logic and visualization taking place in Foundry.
-	When combining structured and “unstructured” assets, the key is utilizing existing metadata and inferring “missing” metadata.
o	Column, row and tabular data is what most implementers mean when they say “structured” data.
o	Assets that are more a string of data points with matrix style references is the better definition of the term “unstructured” (there can be no asset that is truly without structure in a digital medium—there MUST be structure or there is no way to store it).
o	Existing metadata for structured assets are concepts like: schema, logs, approved data ranges, profiles of frequency of values as well as patterns of “drift/shift”.
o	Existing metadata for unstructured assets are generally: steward/owner, location in the system, gps or location information about the asset, revision/replacement history, 
o	Inferring missing metadata is bit more difficult, but also has best practices.
	“Unstructured” assets are actually combinations of structured concepts without explanation as to how they relate. For example, a graphic from a Dashboard in a document, with an image that represents an example of one column in the graphic, and then prose text around the configuration that makes report, analytic and sentiment statements all appear together, used by specific staff, teams or org units, all represent something understood but not document.
	By observing patterns in how all of these assets are combined for repetition, by specific users or teams, and the rate of variation in the statements (report, analysis, sentiment), it is possible to build a time-variant graph analysis and learn the correlation between changes in assets and reconfiguration of the “graph”.
o	Palantir will leverage its own Foundry, Gotham and AIP capabilities to coordinate its internal metadata with any third party processes to assure data is aligned to use cases.
	This is how AIP leverages third-party AI platforms like OpenAI, Claude or others.
	AIP will engage in an orchestration approach to learn the specifications of the data needed by AI Models connecting through it. The metadata from the model on its outputs, inputs, process runs, are then used in AIP to communicate back with Foundry to improve tuning data availability and effectiveness for use in the external AI models. 
	This part is unclear—so what follows is logically sound, but is conjecture rather than reported experience or specifications.
•	That metadata is also added to Gotham, and Gotham used ML and Graph analytics to connect the AI model metadata with other metadata in Gotham. This creates a learning conduit. This does not appear to be properly a “Learning loop”, as everything is sequential and additive as opposed to revision or replacement of preferred metadata for “all” instance metadata. But it does “layer” it.
	AIP has the effect of “replacing” or rendering its own version of MCP, but can also communicate using MCP.
-	Overall cost/benefit advice regarding Palantir versus other solutions.
o	Closed-stacks work well together with best practices coming from direct and significant experience with the solution and significant education and skills curves that are somewhat thwarted by the go-to-market and implementation mod

Discovery Questions (Uncovering Unsanctioned Use)

These should be asked in town halls, team meetings, and one-on-ones with managers and influential employees.

1. To Gauge Awareness & Policy Understanding:

· "When you think of using AI to help with your daily work, what company-approved tools or platforms come to mind?"
· "If you wanted to use an AI tool for a task tomorrow, what would your first step be? Who would you ask?"
· "Have you ever been unsure if a tool or website you were using was powered by AI? How did you resolve that?"

2. To Uncover Usage Without Direct Accusation:

· "To help us provide better tools, what tasks do you find most time-consuming that you wish you could automate or get help with?" (Listen for answers like: summarizing meetings, writing emails, generating code, creating images, analyzing data.)
· "What online tools or websites have you discovered in the last year that have made a significant positive difference in your productivity or creativity?"
· "Have you or your team used any 'productivity assistants,' 'writing aids,' 'code generators,' or 'design helpers' that aren't in our official software catalog? What was the experience like?"
· "When you're stuck on a complex problem, where do you go for help outside of our immediate team?" (Probe if they mention ChatGPT, Copilot, Claude, Perplexity, etc.)

3. To Understand Data Handling Practices (The Critical Risk Question):

· "Imagine you needed to analyze a sensitive project document. How would you use an AI tool to help with that, if at all?"
· "What kind of information would you never put into an online AI tool, and why?"
· "If an AI tool asked for access to your work email or documents to 'provide better context,' what would your gut reaction be?"

Value Assessment Questions (Unlocking Business Potential)

Once you've identified use cases, shift the conversation to value. Target these at teams and individuals who are already using AI.

1. To Quantify Impact:

· "You mentioned using [Tool X] for [Task Y]. Roughly how much time does it save you per week? What do you do with that reclaimed time?"
· "What is the quality of the output like? Does it require heavy editing, or is it largely usable as-is?"

Reference Number: 19361064

Buyer Industry: Banking, Finance and Insurance
Buyer Role: Deputy or Assistant General Counsel - Legal
Purpose: Agentic AI Governance
Question Asked: WR: What are all the aspects that should be included in an Agentic AI Governance guide or checklist?
Analyst Name: Stuart Strome
Discussion Summary: Dear Rubya:

Thank you for your inquiry on the topic of Agentic AI governance. This is an emerging concern for many of our legal and compliance leaders, and we do have a growing body of research on the topic. A few caveats before I provide my response:
I am not sure I can provide you with a comprehensive checklist for Agentic AI governance guide without knowing a bit more about your AI footprint. It best to schedule an in-person discussion so I or a colleague of mine can learn a bit more about Chanel’s agentic AI footprint - but I can provide you with what Gartner tends to suggest as key controls or elements of that checklist.
As I’ll clarify in my written response, because of the nature of agentic AI risk, many of the clients we speak to are opting for more automated, embedded controls solutions. I’ll link to some content below to help you get started with that. However, since that is not my central area of expertise, I will ask that, if you are interested in following up on that, please let either Hannah or Nicole know and they can set you up with the right expert.


What is unique about agentic AI risk:
Before we begin, it’s just important to align on how we define AI agents. A lot of what we see advertised as “agentic” AI does not truly meet the definition and, thus, does not require agentic governance solutions. I will say, this is changing - more companies are pursuing truly agentic capabilities - but just for the sake of clarity: AI agents are defined by autonomous or semi-autonomous abilities. In other words, they can make independent decisions and act autonomously in pursuit of goals. For that reason, a simple deterministic algorithm - think automatically sending a follow-up email in response to an action - would not count since that isn’t truly an independent or autonomous decision. Similarly, a public GenAI tool such as ChatGPT would not count because it cannot independently take action. An example of an AI agent would be a chatbot that can independently process customer returns. 

That being said, AI agents pose similar risks as Generative AI or machine learning systems - data privacy risks, IP leakage, liabilities resulting from errors or biased outcomes, prompt injections - but they also exhibit some unique risks (or at least, uniquely amplified risks. See What GC Need to Know About AI Agent Risks). 
Cascading errors: Because of the autonomy of AI agents, and the fact that a human-in-the-loop erases the efficiency gains agents are designed to produce, there is a high risk of cascading errors. In other words, a poor decision by one agent could go undetected, be amplified and result in a major if not catastrophic risk event.
Goal Misalignment: AI agents may interpret instructions differently than what intended. This could result in actions that are technically correct, but not what the agent’s creator intended. 
Agent Sprawl: Enterprise systems that allow for low or no-code agent development (i.e. Agentforce, Copilot Studio), if left unrestricted, could result in several agents being developed for similar purposes. This agent sprawl makes it difficult to govern (resulting in higher incidence of data breaches, goal misalignment, and could result in inconsistency in outcomes for agent workflows, or even breach of regulatory obligations if an agent’s creator develops one for a high-risk use case per the EU AI Act without the proper controls). 

Key Elements of Agentic AI Governance: If I were developing a checklist for AI agent I would focus on a few elements: 
Ensuring you are taking a risk-based approach - developing a process to identify and place greater scrutiny and more controls on higher-risk use cases for agents.
Keeping an up-to-date database of AI agents along with key information
Assigning accountability for agents’ operations, decisions, and actions 
Managing access and permissions, both for agent creation and agent access to databases a
================================================================================
